# 0.1.7

### Features & Enhancements
- **Dashboard per-model settings are switched on explicitly, and a model's theme settings are edited on a page of their own (`app/pages/settings/dashboard/`, `app/lib/dashboard_builder.lua`, `app/pages/init.lua`, `lib/preferences.lua`, `lib/model_preferences.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `docs/pages/settings/dashboard/`)**:
  - Whether a model uses its own theme and its own theme settings is decided by two switches on *Dashboard* → *Design*: *Allow per-model settings* for the radio, and *Own settings for this model* for the connected model. Before, a theme setting saved while a flight controller was connected went into that model's file and was used by it from then on, with nothing on screen saying so.
  - Theme settings are saved into the scope the page edits instead of wherever a connected flight controller points. The theme tiles under *Dashboard* → *Settings* edit the standard values of every model, and their first line says so. A new *Per-Model Settings* tile, first in that grid and shown while a flight controller is connected and per-model settings are on for its model, opens the settings of each theme that model draws — named by the flight phases it is drawn in — for that model alone, where only a value that differs from the standard is stored; below them it lists what that model changes beside the standard value and resets one or all of them.
  - Switching either switch off keeps the model's theme and theme settings and ignores them; turning *Own settings for this model* off no longer resets the model's theme choice. The switch is also written under the key the former *Model Override* switch used, so an older version on the same card still shows the model's theme.
  - A card written before the switches existed keeps working as it did: an absent radio switch allows per-model settings, and an absent model switch is on for a model that already has a theme or any theme setting of its own. Saving *Design* stores the switches as the page shows them.
  - *Design* and the new page have documentation pages, and the *Settings* page's documentation no longer says that a connected flight controller decides where theme settings are stored.
- **"Main power lost" as a dashboard box source (`widgets/dashboard/objects/common.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - `main_power_lost` is `1` while the main pack is gone and the flight controller is still answering, and `0` otherwise, so a declarative theme can show the state with a plain box and a threshold colour instead of a module of its own. It reads `state.mainPowerLost`, the same answer the *Main power lost* announcement uses.
  - A number and not the boolean, because threshold limits and gauges take numbers and strings only; the same shape as `link_diversity`. It is never `nil`, so before any telemetry it reads `0`, not `--`.
  - It sits at the end of the source list, just before the sensor fall-through, so none of the named sources pays for it; the one comparison is paid by each declared source that falls through to a sensor. `bin/accounting/measure.lua` reports no change on any row.
  - `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md` lists `esc_load` among the sources again and drops a stray half-sentence below the link section, both left by merging the `esc_load` and link-source changes.
- **The flight record counts voltage sags, so the flight log's `sags` and `sag_min` columns are filled (`tasks/events/telemetry/flight_record.lua`, `tasks/events/ondisarm/tasks/flight_log.lua`, `lib/flight_log.lua`, `docs/reference/flight-statistics.md`, `docs/pages/tools/flight_log.md`)**:
  - `sags` and `sag_min` have been in the log's header since it was written and have always been empty, because the suite had no sag detector. A logged flight now says whether the pack went down under load and how far.
  - **A voltage sag** is a dip to or below the flight controller's own minimum cell voltage — its `vbatmincellvoltage`, one physical fact rather than a second threshold of the suite's — counted once per dip, with 0.05 V a cell of hysteresis so that hovering on the line counts once rather than once a sample. A reading at or below 1 V is the main power gone rather than a sag and is not counted, and the deepest per-cell voltage of a sag is kept beside the count on the record as `minSagCellVoltage`.
  - The cell count is the board's, and telemetry's where the board's is set to auto-detect; where neither answers, nothing is judged and the columns stay empty rather than saying zero. The resolution is the record's 0.5 s sampling interval, which is documented.
  - `sags` is `0` where the pack was watched and nothing happened and empty where it could not be watched at all, so a reader can tell the two apart.
  - The two sag keys deliberately get no box `source`: the dashboard's source table maps a live telemetry reading to that reading's recorded extreme, and a sag count is not one. A theme reads them off `state.flight` by name, which is documented with an example.
- **The flight record keeps a headspeed band per PID profile, so the flight log's `hs1_min` … `hs3_max` columns are filled (`tasks/events/telemetry/flight_record.lua`, `tasks/events/ondisarm/tasks/flight_log.lua`, `lib/flight_log.lua`, `docs/reference/flight-statistics.md`, `docs/pages/tools/flight_log.md`)**:
  - `hs1_min` … `hs3_max` have been in the log's header since it was written and have always been empty, because the record kept one headspeed band for the whole flight. A logged flight now says what the head was held at under each PID profile.
  - **Headspeed per PID profile** is `maxRpmP1`/`minRpmP1` to `maxRpmP3`/`minRpmP3` on the record, taken from the profile the flight controller reports while the reading is sampled. Both ends are taken only while the rotor is under power, through the same gate the lowest headspeed and current of a flight are taken through — the flight controller's governor state in `ACTIVE` or `BYPASS`, held for 2 s, with `FALLBACK` excluded — so the pair is the band the governor held rather than the band the spool-up passed through.
  - Where there is no governor state, the same gate's fallback decides, including its wait of 2 s on both sides of a reading; the PID profile a reading was taken on is held back with the reading, so it is booked to that profile rather than to the one the flight controller reports 2 s later. A reconnect clears the profile with the other readings, so a model without that sensor does not book its samples to the last session's profile.
  - The PID profile is one more sensor the record reads on a sampling pass, and it is offered to the dashboard with the others, so the dashboard does not read it a second time in the same pass.
  - Three profiles, because the log has three column pairs; a flight on profile 4, 5 or 6 goes into the overall headspeed extremes like any other reading and into no per-profile key.
  - The per-profile keys deliberately get no box `source`: the dashboard's source table maps a live telemetry reading to that reading's recorded extreme, and "headspeed on PID profile 1" is not a telemetry reading. A theme reads them off `state.flight` by name, which is documented with an example.
- **A theme can draw "main power lost" (`lib/audio.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - The suite could already tell that the main pack is gone while the flight controller is still answering on a BEC or a backup battery, but only inside the *Main power lost* announcement and only while that announcement was switched on. A theme that wants to say so on screen had nothing to read.
  - The three tests behind it are now one function (`Audio.mainPowerLost`), which the announcement calls and which the dashboard calls once per telemetry read, publishing the answer as `state.mainPowerLost`. One definition, so a screen and the voice cannot disagree; and the state field is there whether or not the announcement is enabled.
  - Nothing about the announcement changes: the condition, the 10-second repeat and the call when the pack comes back are what they were, and `mainPowerLostActive` stays what it always was -- a record of what has been spoken, which is why it is not the fact being published.
- **Three dashboard box sources for the radio link itself (`lib/link_rates.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/objects/common.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - `link_packet_rate` names the air rate the link is running at -- `150Hz`, `100Hz Full`, `F1000` -- out of the `RFMD` field of the CRSF link-statistics frame, in the words ExpressLRS itself uses for those rates, for ExpressLRS 3.x and 4.x alike. It is not called `link_rate` because that name is already the flight controller's telemetry link rate in hertz (`session.crsfTelemetryConfig.linkRate`); *ELRS Link* under *Diagnostics* calls this quantity `packetRate` and this follows it.
  - `link_floor` is the receiver sensitivity that rate is specified down to, in dBm, so a theme can turn an RSSI reading into headroom with plain arithmetic instead of a table of its own. The arithmetic stays the theme's; nothing is divided here.
  - `link_diversity` says whether the receiver has a second antenna. It is latched for the flight-controller session and rises only on a reading a second antenna can produce -- a non-zero `2RSS`, or `ANT` naming the second one -- because `ANT` is in every frame and reads a constant 0 on a receiver that has one.
  - ExpressLRS numbers its rates differently in 3.x and 4.x and the frame does not say which, so a theme that declares one of the two rate sources makes the widget send the transmitter module one CRSF device ping; the answer carries its release. Until it has answered, and for a module that is not ExpressLRS 3.x or 4.x, both sources draw `--` rather than a rate the link is not running. Both tables are ExpressLRS's own, cited file by file in `lib/link_rates.lua`; on 3.x the `50Hz` and `250Hz` rates carry no floor, because 3.x gives them one value across two bands with a different sensitivity on each.
  - Nothing is resolved while the link is down: the radio answers every telemetry value with 0 then, and 0 is an air rate.
  - They cost nothing on a theme that names none of them -- no read and no ping -- and one sensor read per telemetry pass on a theme that names one or both of the two rate sources. No shipped theme declares any of the three.
- **The dashboard asks which pack after connecting, and can set that pack's battery profile on the flight controller (`lib/battery_pick.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/battery_pick_menu.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `app/pages/tools/flight_log/`, `docs/pages/tools/flight_log.md`, `docs/dashboard/quick-menu.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - Two new switches on the Flight Log page, both off by default: *Ask which pack after connecting* offers this model's entries from the battery registry once per connection, and *Set the pack's battery profile* lets the pick move the flight controller onto the profile that pack belongs to.
  - The prompt appears **in fullscreen only**, and that is a property of the radio rather than a choice: a widget zone receives no touch and Lua can leave fullscreen but not enter it, so the prompt is what fullscreen shows instead of the quick settings menu until it has been answered or closed, or the model has been armed. Answering it or closing it leaves fullscreen, and the quick menu is what fullscreen shows the next time it is entered, with a *BATTERY* button that brings the picker back while the model is disarmed (over a theme that takes fullscreen, answering or closing it puts the theme back instead); no pack is recorded from the picker while it is armed. *NO BATTERY* always has the picker's bottom row, and packs beyond what fits above it are not offered there. The Flight Log page can still record one of them, but a pick there does not switch the flight controller's battery profile.
  - Which profile a pack belongs to is stated in the registry and inferred nowhere else: `profile=1..6` on the entry wins outright, otherwise the entry's `cap=` must match one of the six capacities the flight controller publishes exactly, and where neither answers **nothing is written** — a guessed profile would move the next flight's low-voltage cut. The write is `MSP_SET_BATTERY_PROFILE` plus the EEPROM write, the same pair the quick menu's battery buttons already send, it is skipped when the board reported that profile on connecting and no profile has been written since, and it is refused while the model is armed. Every one of those exits is logged under `rfsuite.battery_pick`, so a pick that changed nothing says which of the six reasons it was instead of looking broken.
  - The pick itself has one implementation now (`lib/battery_pick.lua`): the Flight Log page and the dashboard's picker record it through the same code, into the session the arm edge reads and into the model's own store, so a pack picked in either place cannot end up recorded differently.
  - **A theme can read the prompt and drive it, and draws no part of it.** The prompt's whole state is published read-only as `state.batteryPick`, and `rfsuite.batteryPick` offers select / dismiss / open to anything else on the radio. Documented in `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`. The picker is drawn by the widget for every theme, so its way out is always there; a press records a request and the runtime performs it on the next pass, so no card write and no MSP queue turn happens inside an LVGL press callback.
- **The dashboard's fullscreen quick menu builds from an entry list (`widgets/dashboard/fullscreen_menu.lua`, `docs/dashboard/quick-menu.md`) (#207)**:
  - The menu's content is a list rather than drawing code. `M.entries(widget)` returns the rows in the vocabulary `app/manifest.lua` already uses for the tool's own menus -- `id`, `title`, `kind`, `visibleWhen`, `press` -- and `M.build(children, widget, entries)` draws whatever list it is given, falling back to its own when the argument is omitted. A future quick action is a row in that list rather than an edit to shared drawing logic.
  - `kind` is what the builder makes of a row: `action` is a single button, `choice` is a title over a grid of options. The battery-profile grid is a `choice` row rather than a special case inside the builder, and each capacity carries its own label, its own highlight and its own press.
  - The in-flight tuning entry's preview guard is now `visibleWhen = "previewInflightTuning"`, resolved by name against the widget of the pass being drawn. A condition name with no resolver hides its row, which is what an unresolvable condition does in `app/menu_registry.lua`. `enabledWhen`, `lockedWhileArmed` and `confirm` are part of the same vocabulary and no entry sets one, so the first entry that needs one brings its resolver with it.
  - **The battery-profile grid is three buttons wide on a screen at least 400 pixels across when the model carries more than four profiles.** With two columns, six profiles need three rows, and one more action row above the grid than the menu has today would push the third row past the bottom edge of an 800x480 screen, where it would be left out without a word. Three columns keep six profiles to two rows. Four profiles or fewer, and narrower screens, keep the grid they had.
  - **Apart from that grid, nothing on screen changes and nothing a press does changes.** The header, the close button, the two layout profiles, the order of the rows and every colour are what they were. What the menu builds was compared node by node, in every property the builder sets, at both layout sizes and with the preview switch on and off, and each press was compared by the jobs it queues and the state it leaves behind.
  - The quick menu has a documentation page for the first time, and the dashboard documentation index points at it.
- **The dashboard's fullscreen surfaces are views on one stack, and what follows a press is data (`widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/fullscreen_menu.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/battery_pick_menu.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`, `docs/dashboard/quick-menu.md`) (#207)**:
  - The quick menu and the battery picker are views in a registry, and which one full screen shows is a bounded stack of view ids on the widget, four deep. It replaces the two flags that decided it before, and the pass that arrives without an event still drops it in one assignment.
  - A view can open itself: the picker declares `openWhen = "batteryPickPending"` and opens while the prompt waits for an answer, ahead of the menu. A view its condition opened closes again once the condition falls, so arming, a pick and a reconnect, which end the prompt without closing anything, still take the picker off the screen.
  - Every entry, option and button carries an `after` action -- `done`, `openView:<id>`, `closeView`, `exitFullscreen` or `none` -- and its `press` does the work only. `views.navigate()` performs the action and is now the one place a view leaves full screen, where there were five copies of the exit in two files.
  - One build step, named after the view, replaces the menu's and the picker's two identical ones. The job kinds stay `menu` and `battery_pick`; the menu's render key is now its id, `menu`.
  - The conditions an entry's `visibleWhen` and a view's `openWhen` name are one list in `views.lua`, moved there from `fullscreen_menu.lua` unchanged, plus `batteryPickPending`. `rfsuite.batteryPick.open()` and `dismiss()` open and close the picker through the same stack; `select()` is unchanged.
  - The stack lies above a base layer, which a theme that takes full screen sets (see the entry for that below); without one, an empty stack shows the quick menu as before.
  - **Nothing a pilot sees or does changes.** The menu and the picker build the same nodes as before, and every press does the same work and leaves full screen where it did. `bin/accounting/measure.lua` reports every row unchanged.
- **A dashboard theme can take fullscreen, with the quick menu and the battery picker opening over it (`widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/fullscreen_controls.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/battery_pick_menu.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`, `docs/dashboard/quick-menu.md`, `docs/pages/tools/flight_log.md`) (#207)**:
  - **A theme that says nothing is not affected.** Its zone, its fullscreen -- the battery prompt or the quick menu -- and every key are what they were, and every row of `bin/accounting/measure.lua`, `--phases` included, reports the same number as before.
  - A theme opts in with `fullscreen = "theme"` in its `init.lua`. Fullscreen then builds that theme at the fullscreen size as the base layer under the view stack, in a job of its own (`fs_theme`), under the same 2 Hz render-key throttle as the zone and with its own key, computed at once on the pass the theme comes back on screen so that an entry builds it once rather than twice. The quick menu and the battery picker open over it, and `done` -- the menu's X, its entries, a pick and the picker's X -- puts the theme back instead of leaving fullscreen.
  - A free-form theme's `build(zone, state, ctx)` receives `ctx` in fullscreen: `ctx.action(after)`, `ctx.keys` (actions for RTN and the two page keys while the theme shows), `ctx.condition(name)`, and the quick menu's `ctx.entries()` and builder `ctx.menu(children, entries)`. The theme decides which controls it shows and what they do, including whether one leaves fullscreen; the documentation names what it owes -- a way into the menu, and a way out or a stated reliance on a long press on RTN.
  - A fullscreen tree that binds no press anywhere -- always the case for a declarative theme -- gets the widget's two controls on top of it: a menu glyph and an X where the quick menu's X is, which leaves fullscreen. The glyph's bars are `line` nodes, because a `rectangle` built in fullscreen takes the press itself and would swallow it.
  - In this mode the widget answers the page keys (open or close the menu) and a short RTN (the view on top closes, the picker as its close box does), read ahead of the job a pass may run so that a key is not lost to a build. The keys are read from `EVT_VIRTUAL_NEXT_PAGE`, `EVT_VIRTUAL_PREV_PAGE` and `EVT_VIRTUAL_EXIT`, on their release edges only. A widget whose theme did not opt in answers no key, as before.
  - `batteryPickPending` is false once a pick has been recorded, not only once it has been applied, so a picker answered over a theme does not open again in the passes before the pick is applied.
  - What it costs, counted offline with the accounting stubs, same world and theme, against the tree before: a legacy fullscreen pass +7 instructions with the menu up and +11 with the picker up, and +139 on the first fullscreen pass after a theme change, which reads `init.lua` for the key; zone passes unchanged. A theme-mode pass with the theme showing +22 over the legacy menu pass, plus the theme's own render key at 2 Hz (90 for a copy of `default`); with the menu or the picker over the theme +43 and +36. The fullscreen build of a copy of `default` peaks at 5626 instructions per pass at 800x480.
  - The battery prompt's documentation says what a pick and the close box do in both cases (fixes #454).
- **A dashboard box can show ESC load (`widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/objects/common.lua`, `app/pages/setup/esc_motors/esc_tools/esc_current_limit.lua`, `app/pages/setup/power/preferences/page.lua`, `docs/pages/setup/power/preferences.md`)**:
  - New box source `esc_load`: the current as a percentage of the current limit the speed controller is set to allow. It is a figure a pilot can judge without knowing what the controller is rated for, which a reading in amps is not, and a value above 100 % says the controller is being asked for more than it allows.
  - The limit is not asked for where it can be read. AM32, Scorpion and YGE carry it in their own parameter block, and opening that family's page under *ESC Tools* -- which reads the block anyway -- stores it for that flight controller, in amps, in the same step. Nothing is asked of the flight controller for it, nothing is added to the connect sequence, and a visit that finds the same limit writes nothing.
  - The other seven ESC families report no limit, so *Setup* → *Power* → *Preferences* gains an *ESC Current Limit* row for it. It defaults to *NOT SET*, and with no limit on file an `esc_load` box shows `--` rather than a reading.
  - It costs one division on a pass that has both a limit and a current reading, and a pair of comparisons on a pass that has neither; no telemetry sensor is read for it.
  - Those three ESC pages also gain the in-app help text behind the `?` that they were shipped without.
- **The dashboard computes two further flight phases, `armed` and `offline` (`widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/dashboard/user-themes.md`)**:
  - `armed` is the model armed and not yet flying — from the arm until the governor comes up or the throttle passes its threshold. It used to be reported as `preflight`, so the ground screen covered both a model sitting on the bench and a model that is live with the blades turning.
  - `offline` is the post-flight phase once the flight controller has stopped answering. The widget already stopped reading telemetry there so the summary would not decay; the phase now says so, which lets a theme distinguish a model still on the link and armable from one whose numbers can no longer change. What causes it — an unplugged battery, the model out of range, the link gone — the widget cannot tell apart, and the documentation says so rather than naming one of them.
  - **Both are refinements and both are optional for a theme.** A theme declares `armed` and `offline` in its `init.lua` if it wants to draw them; without those keys `armed` resolves to the theme's preflight module and `offline` to its postflight one. Every theme in this repository is unchanged and needs no new file, and the theme selection keeps its three slots — `armed` resolves through the preflight slot, `offline` through the postflight one.
  - **A phase change that resolves to the same module no longer rebuilds the scene.** A rebuild tears the LVGL tree down and builds it again eight boxes per pass, which for a theme that draws the two new phases with its existing modules would have been a rebuild for no visible change — once per arm, and again on every link transition after a flight. Both places that could cause it now go by the module rather than by the phase name: the reload decision in the widget, and the engine's render key, which is built from the new `state.themePhase` instead of `state.flightMode`. Measured on `bin/accounting` against the unchanged tree: the armed scenario builds the scene twice on both, and the worst armed pass — the arm edge — goes from 11956 to 11971 against a target of 12200, with no extra rebuild behind it. The arm edge stays that cheap because nothing on it resolves again: the theme path is memoised by the selection slot, which `armed` shares with `preflight`, and the module every phase resolves to is worked out once when the theme loads, so the phase change compares one lookup.
  - The phase table in `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md` said arming alone does not leave preflight. That was true and is not any more; the page, that page's own release note in this section and `docs/dashboard/user-themes.md` are corrected in the same change.
- **A dashboard box can show what the speed controller says about itself (`lib/esc_status.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/objects/common.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `docs/reference/esc-status.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - Four new box sources, which are two pairs. In each pair the first is a translated string -- *OK*, *OVERHEAT*, *THROTTLE LOST*, *BEC UNDERVOLTAGE* -- and the second is 1, 2 or 3 so a theme can colour the line, with no value at all where nothing answered. `esc_status`/`esc_status_level` are the worst reading since the flight controller connected; `esc_status_live`/`esc_status_live_level` are what the controller is saying on this pass. Naming either half of a pair declares both halves of it, because they are one reading; the two pairs stand alone.
  - The flight controller already sends the two values this needs, *ESC1 Status* and *ESC1 Model*, and until now nothing read them. The status word has no common layout -- each manufacturer's protocol assigns its own bits -- so the model byte is what makes the word readable at all, and `lib/esc_status.lua` now holds one table per protocol: Kontronik's 24 error and warning flags, Hobbywing V5's eight fault codes, Scorpion's six, Graupner's seven warnings, APD's five, and OpenYGE, whose byte is a motor state and a warning code sharing one byte rather than a flag word.
  - **The five signatures that fill no status word report *NO STATUS*, not *OK*.** Hobbywing V4, BLHeli32, BLHeli_S, AM32 and Castle leave the word at zero, and reading that zero as health would be a claim nothing supports. FlyRotor, XDFly, OMP Hobby and ZTW do fill it but publish no layout, so all that is reported for them is that something is being reported.
  - Severity follows each protocol's own wording: *error* or *protection* is a fault, *warning* or *limit reached* is a warning, and where a protocol calls its whole field warning flags every bit in it is a warning. OpenYGE's three state-dependent codes are read the way its own description makes them -- a warning while the motor runs, a fault with the power cut.
  - **Both readings exist because "now" and "this flight" are different questions, and neither answers the other.** A status line a pilot reads as *what is wrong with my machine* has to follow the controller down as well as up, or it holds an alarm the controller has withdrawn; a value row read as *what this flight has seen* has to keep the worst, or it loses the fault that ended the flight. A controller that cut power for half a second and recovered has said why the flight ended, and a dashboard pass is slower than a fault -- so that reading goes on file until the widget starts a new session with a flight controller. A request to restart the controller is never put on file: the flight controller withdraws it on the next telemetry frame, so it belongs to the live reading alone and a fault already on file outranks it.
  - It costs nothing on a theme that names none of the four: there is no new sensor read, no new state field and nothing in the connect sequence. A theme that does name one pays two sensor reads per telemetry pass and reads the layout again only when one of the two bytes moved -- and one decode answers all four, so a theme showing a live line above a recorded row pays that once.
  - Neither sensor is selected on most flight controllers. *Diagnostics* → *Validate Sensors* lists them under those two labels, and with neither on the radio the box draws `--`.
- **A dashboard theme may declare the telemetry it reads (`widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `lib/sensors.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/reference/sensor-selection.md`)**:
  - A phase module may carry a `sources` list beside its `boxes` or its `build` function, and the widget resolves everything in it into `state.derived` on the telemetry-read cadence, exactly as it already does for the sources its boxes name. It is the only way a **free-form** theme reaches anything beyond the fixed state fields -- it declares no boxes, so there was nothing for the widget to walk -- and it is what lets one tile of a declarative theme draw a session minimum or maximum beside its live value, which is three readings through a field that holds one.
  - The list is the phase module's own, so it is per phase by construction rather than by a new setting: the widget loads only the module for the phase it is drawing and rebuilds the list on every phase change, so telemetry a post-flight screen needs is not read in the air. `sources` may also be a function returning the list, called once per theme load like `boxes`, which is how a theme whose settings page chooses what to show builds it from its own configuration.
  - Anything in the list may be a telemetry sensor of the radio's under its own name, including the radio's per-sensor session minimum and maximum (`Hspd+`, `RQly-`). A name the model has no sensor for is searched for less and less often instead of on every pass, and a theme that declares nothing pays nothing: the list is only walked where a module carries one, and no declared source enters the render key, so a value changing redraws rather than rebuilding the scene.
  - **The link statistics now have an entry in the sensor catalogue** -- `1RSS`, `2RSS`, `RSNR`, `ANT`, `RFMD`, `TPWR`, `TRSS`, `TQly`, `TSNR` and the four extended-frame rows `RRSP`, `RPWR`, `TRSP`, `TFPS` -- with the unit and the precision the radio creates them with. They are the radio's own sensors rather than the flight controller's, so no table in the suite described them: `lib/rf2tlm_sensors.lua` covers the custom telemetry and these come from the CRSF driver. A theme that names one as a source can now ask `Sensors.getMetadata` what unit it is in.
  - **Corrected `RQly` in the same table, which said `dB`.** It is a percentage -- the radio declares that sensor `UNIT_PERCENT` where it creates it -- and `TQly`, the same quantity in the other direction, is now beside it saying so.
- **A connection status button in the header (`ui/header.lua`, `ui/home.lua`, `tasks/events/common/runner.lua`, `docs/reference/connection-status.md`)**:
  - The start screen leaves as soon as the flight controller has answered the API version and the unique id, or the wait runs out, and the compile pass has walked the installed tree; the connect sequence then runs on behind the menu with nothing on screen saying so. A flight controller that never answered and one whose configuration is still being read looked the same from the outside, and they are the two a pilot most needs to tell apart. The header now carries a button whose symbol names the state -- no telemetry link, connecting, connected, or something went wrong -- and pressing it opens the detail: which of the two connect phases is running and which step, the reason a handshake failed, or the craft name with the Rotorflight and MSP API versions.
  - It is on every screen the header is on, not only on the main menu. The state matters on a page as much as on the menu: a save refused because the flight controller stopped answering is read on the page it was refused on.
  - **Nothing was moved.** The start screen leaves when it always did, and a tile is enabled when it always was. The button reports the state; it does not gate on it.
  - A connect step that is given up on still counts towards the sequence being finished, so a bare "connected" would have covered a connection missing part of its configuration. `getProgress()` now reports that count beside the total, the button shows the warning symbol, and the notice opens with *Connect steps not completed: n*. It reports the pending task off the same walk, so reporting progress no longer costs two walks of the queue.
  - **A state change rebuilds nothing.** The button's symbol is a text function the firmware resolves on its own refresh pass, and the notice is composed at the press, so nothing is formatted for a state nobody asks about. The connect runner is not looked at again once the sequence is through.
  - One log line per state change, under the `rfsuite.home` tag.
- **A per-flight-controller settings file says which helicopter it belongs to (`lib/model_preferences.lua`, `tasks/events/onconnect/tasks/name.lua`, `docs/reference/configuration-files.md`) (#197)**:
  - The store on the card is named after the board's MCU id and carried nothing a reader could recognise, so a card full of them could only be told apart by hex. The model name the flight controller reports is now written into the file as `craft.name` while a board is connected, which is what #197 asked for.
  - It is written only where it differs from what the file already says, so an ordinary connect costs the comparison and neither a rewrite of the file nor the reload signal a save sends to the widgets. It is written only in a Lua state that may write — the configuration tool and the background telemetry decoder — which is the test the settling write in the same file already makes, because a widget call is cut off at a fixed instruction count.
  - **A flight controller with no name set leaves no trace in the file.** The read answers with an empty string rather than with nothing, and storing a placeholder for it would be indistinguishable from a craft actually called that — the next connect would then find a name present and never correct it. The key is declared as optional rather than with a default for the same reason: a default is written into every model file on every save.
  - No existing file is rewritten for this. A store that predates the release still loads complete, because an optional key that is absent is not a missing declaration.
- **The per-flight-controller stores on the card can be enumerated with nothing connected (`lib/model_preferences.lua`)**:
  - `ModelPreferences.listKnownModels()` answers the stores the card holds — the board id, the stored name where there is one, the file and the root it was found in — without asking a flight controller anything. Until now every entry point into that store needed an MCU id, which exists only while a board is connected, so the settings belonging to a machine could not be reached without the machine.
  - It costs one directory listing per user root and one file read per known board, which is a tool-session cost and not something to put in a widget pass or a wakeup. It caches nothing.
  - It writes to the card under no circumstances, including the one where a save was interrupted: finishing that rename is a write, and a call that only lists may not make one.
  - A radio whose firmware offers no directory enumeration answers an empty list rather than an error, and the files the suite itself keeps beside the stores are not mistaken for one.
- **A dashboard theme may split its settings into pages, each with its own icon (`app/pages/settings/dashboard/lib.lua`, `app/lib/dashboard_builder.lua`, `app/pages/settings/dashboard/settings/page.lua`, `bin/package/build_package.py`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/pages/settings/dashboard/settings.md`)**:
  - A theme whose settings outgrow one screen declares `pages` in its `init.lua`, and its tile under *System* → *Settings* → *Dashboard* → *Settings* then opens a grid of those pages instead of the settings page itself. Each tile opens the theme's own `configure.lua` with the page named on the context — on the factory's and on every `build`, `onReload` and `onSave` call, because a module returned as a plain table never sees a factory context. A theme that declares nothing, or fewer than two usable entries, behaves exactly as before: one tile, one page.
  - A page carries its own icon, relative to the theme folder. Icons are looked up before use and fall back down a chain — the page's icon, the theme's `icon.png`, the tool's settings icon — so a theme that ships none draws the tool's icon rather than an empty tile. **A configurable theme's tile now shows the theme's `icon.png`**, which the theme list had always computed and nothing had ever drawn.
  - The generated `theme_index.lua` carries the declared pages as well, so the split is there on a radio whose firmware offers no directory enumeration and the theme list is read from the index. The packager's parser for it has a `--self-test`, since no theme in this repository declares pages and a packaging run over the tree alone would prove nothing about it.
  - The shipped themes are unchanged; none of them has settings enough to want a split.
- **Dashboard themes are documented at source level (`docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - Added the contributor guide for building a dashboard theme: where a theme is found and how its `<source>/<folder>` path is formed, the `init.lua` key set, the declarative and free-form module shapes with their fallback chain, the `layout` grid and the box vocabulary with every object type and subtype the tree ships, the `configure.lua` factory and the `cfg_<theme>_<key>` prefix that keeps a copied theme from writing over the original, and the checklist a theme shipped in this repository has to satisfy.
  - **What puts the widget into each flight phase is written down for the first time.** The phase is computed per pass from the flight controller's telemetry, not chosen by the theme or the pilot: arming alone does not put the model in flight, inflight is reached by an active governor, by throttle above its threshold, or — with the governor off — by headspeed, current or throttle above the direct-drive thresholds, and the latch that holds inflight until disarm is what makes a landing produce a postflight screen and a bench arm not produce one. The page states the readings and the numbers, that a phase change is a full theme reload rather than a redraw, and that the postflight screen deliberately outlives the link.
  - Documented two things a theme author otherwise finds by the screen going blank: a box's `source` is collected as a string literal when the theme loads, so a `source` given as a function resolves against a snapshot that does not carry it; and a value given as a function is resolved either once at build time or per frame in the firmware's reactive sweep, where the reactive-closure rule applies to a theme's own closures exactly as it applies to the object modules.
  - The dashboard and developer indexes point at it, and `docs/dashboard/user-themes.md` links it for the detail it does not carry.
  - **Corrected `docs/dashboard/user-themes.md`, which sent a theme author to folders that do not exist.** It named the shipped themes under `/SCRIPTS/TOOLS/rfsuite/` rather than `/SCRIPTS/TOOLS/rfsuite-core/`, put a user theme one level too deep under `rfsuite.user/dashboard/themes/` instead of `rfsuite.user/dashboard/`, and said a user theme overrides a shipped one of the same name — both folders are listed, so a copy that keeps the original's name appears in the selector twice. The `init.lua` bullet also listed an author and display profiles among its keys; the loader reads neither.
- **The flight log records each flight's statistics (`tasks/events/ondisarm/tasks/flight_log.lua`, `tasks/events/telemetry/flight_record.lua`, `docs/pages/tools/flight_log.md`) (fixes #250)**:
  - The log has always declared columns for a flight's capacity, voltages, current, ESC temperature and BEC voltage, and has always written them empty because nothing could reach the numbers. They are filled now, from the flight record the event runtimes keep, so a line in the log says what the flight actually did rather than only how long it lasted.
  - Per-cell voltage uses the flight controller's battery cell count; where that has not been read, those two columns stay empty rather than being divided by a guess. The headspeed-per-profile and voltage-sag columns stay empty, because the suite records neither yet.
  - A flight that produced no statistics at all is still written as the five-field line it has always been, rather than as a line of empty columns.
  - The flight log now has a documentation page, which is also the first page under Tools to get one.
- **Flight statistics are recorded without a dashboard on screen (`tasks/events/telemetry/flight_record.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `docs/reference/flight-statistics.md`) (fixes #249)**:
  - The extremes of a flight and its armed time are kept by the event runtimes rather than by a dashboard widget, and published under `rfsuite.session.flight`. A model that does not use the dashboard -- one running the service widget so that other widgets have the MSP surface -- now records its flights all the same, and a dashboard placed afterwards shows the last one.
  - A flight now starts and ends on one definition of the arm edge, the flight controller's arm flag as the event runtimes read it, which is the edge the flight log and the post-disarm reads already fire on. The record is closed first of everything on that edge, so a task behind it reads a finished flight.
  - The flight clock advances on every wakeup instead of only on a pass in which telemetry changed, so a hover with the numbers standing still is no longer missing from the flight time. A single step of it is capped at one second, because a widget can be suspended for a whole tool session.
  - **The total flight time and the flight count now come from the flight controller, and a tile showing either will jump.** The total used to be the armed seconds the dashboard widget instance had counted since it was built: never stored, so it restarted at zero each time; undercounted, because its clock only ran in a pass where telemetry had changed; and separate per placement. It is now the board's own lifetime total for the model (`stats_total_time_s`), plus the flight in progress while armed -- so a pilot who saw this session's minutes sees the machine's hours. The board adds a flight only once its armed time passes the board's own minimum (`stats_min_armed_time_s`, 15 s by default), so the totals can fall by a very short flight when the board is read again after the disarm; that is the board's definition and it is documented rather than worked around. Where the board has not answered, the suite falls back to its own count.
  - A theme reading the old flat field names off the widget state (`currentFlightMaxRpm`, `lastMinVoltage` and the rest) keeps working: those names are mapped onto the record. They are deprecated in favour of `state.flight` and the box `stattype`.
  - Added an offline accounting scenario that is **armed** and whose sensors **move** between passes, with rows `pass.state.armed` and `unit.events.wakeup.armed`. Every other steady-state row of that instrument is measured disarmed against a frozen sensor set, so none of them can see what a flight costs.
- **Background Custom-Telemetry Decoder (`SCRIPTS/FUNCTIONS/rfsbg.lua`)**:
  - Added an optional special-function script that runs the custom-telemetry drain and the adjustment announcements in the radio's script state, where a long call is yielded rather than cut off at a fixed instruction count, so every queued frame is decoded instead of only the newest few.
  - While the script is taking frames off the wire it publishes a moving counter in a shared-memory slot; the dashboard and service widgets read it and skip their own drain and announcements for as long as it moves, and resume within one second if it stops (a tool session, a radio without the special function, or another permanent script consuming the frames first).
  - Extracted the drain into `tasks/events/telemetry_bg/drain.lua` so both hosts run the same decoder, frame accounting and publish-on-change throttle.
  - The suite adds that special function itself to a model that does not carry it, and decides by reading the model's own special-function slots. It keeps no record of its own, so it writes nothing to the preferences and asks the flight controller nothing.
  - A model that already runs another background decoder from a special function (Rotorflight's earlier Lua suite installs `rf2bg`) is left alone: every special-function script shares one telemetry queue, so a second decoder would occupy one of the radio's few script slots and receive nothing. The reason is written to the log, and the dashboard decodes for itself exactly as it does on a radio without the script.
  - The script now writes to the card like the tool and the widgets do while *Log Session To Card* is on, under a `function_` prefix of its own: a session file on the same cadence, and a one-line step file that is closed on the spot and carries the Lua heap figure it used to print only at *TRACE*. It is the one part of the suite that keeps writing when the widgets do not — a widget whose pass overruns its instruction budget is not called again and so cannot record that it stopped, while this script is yielded and resumed — so its step file's timestamp is what says how far a radio that stopped answering got. What it writes is a debug record and is gated like one: it needs *Log Session To Card* on **and** a debug level of *DEBUG* or higher, the same condition the rest of the suite writes under, so nothing appears in the log directory while the level says otherwise. Two things then keep it off a radio that is not using it: the sink module is loaded the first time both are found set and never before, so a pilot who leaves them alone pays nothing for it in the script state's Lua memory; and the script takes the longer of the sink's two flush cadences unconditionally rather than reading the arm sensor for it, so it puts no further module into that state either. Documented in `docs/troubleshooting/collecting-logs.md`.
  - **The script now also watches the dashboard widget and records the moment it stops.** A widget that overruns the per-cycle instruction budget is not called again, so it can never write down that this happened to it, and until now nothing on the card could. The dashboard publishes a pass counter and its previous pass's instruction usage into one of EdgeTX's shared-memory variables — the one channel there is between two Lua states — and the background decoder, which is yielded rather than cut off, notices the counter standing still and writes `dashboard widget silent 3.0 s, last pass 56 %`. It reports once per stop, never while the counter is moving, and never for a radio that has simply never had the dashboard on screen. The publish costs 17 instructions on the passes it happens, against a per-pass budget of 12200, and is behind the same debug level as everything else that writes.
  - **The widget's own step file now names the class of work the pass was doing** — *splash*, *scene*, *menu* or a tuning surface — instead of standing frozen at whichever task the connect sequence last ran, which is worse than an empty file because it looks like an answer. One line per job pass, with the constant key the module's throttle needs: measured on a card, 32 writes over the first 25 seconds of connecting and building and none at all over 32 seconds of render load afterwards, against a throttle ceiling of four a second that is never reached.
- **Audio: telemetry lost and recovered, the voltage spoken, main power lost (`lib/audio.lua`, `Settings > Audio > Events`)**:
  - The low-voltage alert now speaks the pack voltage it fired at, and a new *Hold (s)* setting (0-10 s, default 2) makes the reading stay below the warning level for that long before anything is said, so a pack sagging under a hard collective pull is no longer announced as a pack that is down. The level itself is unchanged: it stays the flight controller's own `vbatwarningcellvoltage`.
  - New *Main Power Lost* announcement on the Voltage page (default off), for a setup with a backup guard or a separate receiver battery: the main pack reads as gone while a BEC voltage is still there, repeated every 10 seconds with the BEC voltage spoken and once more, with the pack voltage, when the pack comes back.
  - New *Telemetry Lost* announcement on the Link page (default off): a model lost while it was armed, and again when it answers. Only a flight controller that stops answering while the radio link is still up is announced, so it cannot double up with the radio's own telemetry-lost callout; a drop while disarmed is a normal power-off and stays silent.
  - Every spoken value goes through the radio's own `playNumber`, so the digits and the unit follow the language set on the radio.
  - Documented in `docs/audio/events.md`, which now covers every announcement category.
- **Audio: repeat and haptic are properties of an alert category (`lib/audio.lua`, `Settings > Audio > Events`)**:
  - Every alert that reports a condition now takes two settings from its category page: *Repeat*, which is *Until cleared* or a count from 1 to 10, and *Haptic*, which decides whether the transmitter vibrates with the voice. That is one pair per page rather than one pair per announcement.
  - The pair covers the low voltage, main power lost and BEC / receiver alerts on the Voltage page, the link quality and lost telemetry announcements on the Link page, the ESC and MCU temperature alerts on the ESC page, and the empty alarm on the Fuel page.
  - The two settings the Fuel page already had (`fuel_repeat_below_zero`, `fuel_haptic_below_zero`) are those keys, so an existing `preferences.ini` reads exactly as it did; the other categories default to the behaviour they had, which is *Until cleared* with the haptic on.
  - Seven alerts buzzed with the vibration written into the code and no way for the pilot to switch it off. There is now one place that vibrates, and the pilot decides per category whether it does.
  - Fixed: the main power alert's repeat interval was the only one not cleared when a model reconnects, so the first announcement of a new connection could be held back by up to ten seconds by the previous one.
  - Documented in `docs/audio/events.md` under *Repeat and Haptic*.
- **Audio: Adaptive WAV Volume and Master-Volume Bridge (`Settings > Audio > Volume`, `lib/audio.lua`, `docs/pages/settings/audio/volume.md`) (fixes #172)**:
  - **Layer A (Adaptive WAV Volume):** Configure a fixed WAV volume level (1..5) for all RFSuite speech announcements, or keep "Radio default" (0). An optional "Connected only" switch restricts the volume override to active flights, keeping bench and menu work at the transmitter's own volume.
  - **Layer B (Master Volume Bridge):** Dynamically drives a model Global Variable (GVAR) to control overall transmitter volume via EdgeTX Special Functions and Inputs. Automatically applies a configured *Normal* level during flight, boosts to *Alert* level during sustained critical alarms (Low Voltage, ESC Over-Temperature, Empty Fuel/Battery), and drops back to `-1024` (sentinel) when disconnected or disarmed so transmitter pot control is cleanly restored.
  - New dedicated settings page under `Settings > Audio > Volume` with a warning when the chosen GVAR has popup enabled.
- **In-Flight Tuning (preview) (`widgets/dashboard/inflight`, `settings/dashboard/inflight`, `setup/controls/inflight`)**:
  - Added an in-flight tuning surface to the dashboard widget as a **preview feature**, hidden until it is switched on under `Settings > General > Preview`. One key covers every way in: both menu entries carry it as their visibility condition, the widget builds no drive and reads no file of the overlay without it, and the Quick Settings offer the way into the surface only while it is on.
  - A pilot-chosen interlock switch is the only entry, and what it shows follows the flight rather than the switch: on the ground before a flight the setup check and the values read from the flight controller, in the air one adjustment parameter at a time with bank chips, a row list, the value it had a step ago and the value it started the flight with, and step controls; on the ground after a flight the list of what changed and the restore. The step controls and the trims are live in the air and nowhere else.
  - The flight controller is driven through its own adjustment functions, by pulsing two declared EdgeTX global variables onto the enable and value channels. A press writes the row's magnitude for at least the configured pulse length however early it is let go, which is what the board's 100 ms trigger delay and 200 ms repeat delay ask for: the plateau has to outlast the one and end before the other, which is exactly one step. The pulse is **180 ms by default, settable from 100 to 250**. What the board sees is the setting plus up to one widget pass, since the pulse is cleared on the first pass at or after its deadline, plus the link's own jitter -- so 180 leaves about 40 ms to each edge, and 250 is the upper bound because it is the largest setting whose overrun alone cannot reach the repeat at 300 ms. Trims are pulses in the same way, in either of two layouts belonging to the radio: walk and adjust, which claims up to three trims, or one trim per row, the documented six-band layout.
  - Works while armed by construction: everything the overlay does in the air is a global variable write, so it is unaffected by the armed queue clear. Every write is recorded before it is made, and the value variable is taken back to zero on every exit path -- interlock released, fullscreen closed, widget backgrounded, link lost, and a held step control let go before the rebuild that would delete the button reporting its release -- including a clear made from the widget entry point while the widget is backed off after an instruction-limit fault, during which none of the paths inside the widget runs. Both variables are cleared in the flight mode they were written in, since the radio resolves a "same as FMx" link on the write itself.
  - The MSP half is a separate module that runs on the ground only. It refuses while the flight controller is armed, while the rotor is turning and while the arm sensor has not been read at all -- a model whose arm sensor is not in the telemetry list reads as disarmed for ever, so that case is named on screen rather than treated as safe.
  - On the ground the overlay primes itself over MSP: the receiver map, the board's own adjustment slot table and nine value reads that between them answer every adjustment function, once per connect or on demand. In the **Standard** set layout the six banks of six parameters are the layout the project's own generic radio setup documents, named before the flight controller has said anything, and the board's slot table is read to be held against that set; in **Custom** the set is whatever the board carries, derived from the enable and increment windows of the slots on this model's declared channels, and nothing is ever written to it.
  - Undo for a flight: the ground screen copies the flying PID profile into a spare one and restores it afterwards, using the same MSP pair as the Copy Profiles tool, and takes that backup by itself when the interlock is switched on with the machine on the ground, the link up and the values read. After a flight that moved something the overlay lists every parameter that has left the snapshot, largest change first, paged with the trim that walks the parameters in the air, with a footer saying that the flight controller has already written all of it to its own storage.
  - The overlay follows the PID and rate profiles the flight controller is flying, since the firmware's adjustment functions act on the active profile: a profile change marks every cached value belonging to that profile as unknown and names the new profile on the screen, the nine value reads are sent again once the profile switch has stood still, and a restore into a profile other than the one the backup was taken from is refused.
  - Added `Settings > Dashboard > In-Flight Tuning` for the radio's half -- the master switch, the interlock switch, the enable and value channels and global variables, the pulse length and the trims -- with **Set up the model**, which builds a plan of the mixer lines, variable details and trim modes it would change, puts it up for confirmation and only then writes, in the order that keeps every intermediate state safe. The page reads the model back and reports what is missing, including a bank and a value pointed at one variable or one channel and one trim claimed by two rows. **These settings live with the radio**, in its own preferences file, and apply to every model on it; no flight controller has to be connected to change them.
  - Added `Setup > Controls > In-Flight Tuning`, beside Adjustments, for the flight controller's half -- **a switch of the model's own**, set layout, step size and backup PID profile, all of them stored with the model. **Both switches have to be on before anything drives**: the radio's is the master, so with it off nothing happens on any model, and the model's decides which of the machines on that radio the overlay is set up for. The page carries **Set up the flight controller**, which reads the board in two replies, puts up what each slot holds and what it would hold, writes one adjustment range per slot on confirmation followed by a single EEPROM write, and reads every written slot back field by field. The page is gated on the link and locked while armed like its neighbours, and each of the two pages names the other.
  - Added a per-model **step size** of 1, 2, 5 or 10, which is what one press moves a parameter by on the board, and a second per-model **head speed step** of 10, 25, 50 or 100, defaulting to 50, which reaches the governor head speed alone -- its range is 0 to 10000 rpm where no other parameter of the set is bounded above 250, so the step that makes a gain move by a feelable amount would take thousands of presses to cross it. Both are written into the slots the setup action puts there, so a changed step means the flight controller has to be set up again, which the board comparison reports by name.
  - Added offline instruction accounting rows `pass.tuning.state`, `pass.tuning.prime` and `pass.job.tuning` with measured figures, and the switch, global variable, mixer and flight-mode surface the accounting stubs needed for them. The ground half has a row of its own because the pass an MSP reply arrives in pays that reply's parse, and at most one reply is parsed per pass.
  - The model's setup check now **gates the overlay** instead of only describing it. It walks the mixer lines, the global variable details and the trim modes, and it names the conditions under which a press would move something other than the parameter on the screen -- a bank and a value pointed at one variable or one channel, a missing or wrong mixer line, a variable whose range or precision cannot carry the codes, a trim the active flight mode still moves the stick neutral with. A **fault refuses the interlock**: nothing goes live and no variable is written, and the zone surface comes up with the fault named rather than the switch doing nothing silently. An **unknown verdict does not refuse** -- a radio whose script seam cannot answer those questions returns no verdict at all, and a check that could not run is not a check that failed. The model is walked at most once per settings change and once per interlock closing, and never in the air, so a model repaired with the interlock still on is re-checked by switching it off and on.
  - The settings help names the ExpressLRS precondition: Wide switch mode or a full-resolution packet rate, since Hybrid mode's 16- and 6-position channels miss several of the decoded windows.
  - Both settings pages lay their explanatory notes out for the room they take. A label narrower than its text wraps rather than clipping, and the pages advanced by a constant after each note, so the longest of them was drawn over by the line that follows it. The action button on the flight controller page is wider for the same reason: a button label is clipped rather than wrapped, and 240 pixels cut a character off each end of its English text.
- **Urban, a free-form dashboard theme shipped as a demonstration (`widgets/dashboard/themes/urban/`, `app/pages/settings/dashboard/theme_index.lua`, `docs/dashboard/urban-theme.md`, `docs/dashboard/README.md`)**:
  - A worked example of two theme features of this release: it takes fullscreen (`fullscreen = "theme"`, `fullscreenExit = "longRtn"`, one menu button bound through `ctx`), and its flight screen declares the telemetry it reads through a `sources` function built from the value rows the pilot chose. It is a demonstration and not a finished theme; what it does not do yet is listed on its documentation page.
  - One flight screen for the ground and the air -- top bar with the clock, stacked link bars and the radio battery, a left panel with the model, totals, governor, throttle, a status line and the profile numbers, a battery gauge, five value rows chosen by the pilot, and a bottom bar -- and a statistics screen after the flight that reads the flight record: latest, minimum and maximum of cell voltage, headspeed per PID profile, current, ESC temperature, BEC voltage and voltage sags.
  - Its settings are three pages (*Look*, *Value Rows*, *Top Bar*): a light or dark colour scheme, the arm-state colours, the five rows, units, temperature colours, the clock, each link bar, the radio battery, the transmitter power and the two link warning steps.
  - Its words come from the suite's translation files, as the shipped themes' do, so each locale's package draws it in its own language. Its `theme.urban` row in `bin/accounting/budgets.lua` is measured with the accounting fix that settles a free-form theme; its build is the expensive half and its sweep the cheap one, so the target sits above the shipped themes' 14 000 with the margin `pass.job.build` carries.
- **The battery picker draws a record of the quick menu's instead of carrying its own logic (`widgets/dashboard/fullscreen_menu.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/battery_pick_menu.lua`, `docs/dashboard/quick-menu.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`)**:
  - The quick menu's BATTERY row is now the battery prompt's one record: a `choice` whose options are the picks -- one per pack, then *NO BATTERY*, each recording the pick and followed by `done` -- and whose `close` ends the prompt for this connection. The menu draws it as the button that opens the picker, as before, because the record names the picker as the `view` that draws its options.
  - The picker builds from that record, so what a pick and the close box do is written once, in `fullscreen_menu.lua`, and `battery_pick_menu.lua` is the drawing of it. `M.entry(widget, id)` and `M.run(widget, entry, option)` are the two calls it uses: find an entry, and run its work or an option's followed by the action that follows it. Each entry has a builder of its own, so `M.entry` builds the one record asked for rather than the whole list: the picker's build still costs 219 instructions more than before on re-entering fullscreen (372 on the first, which also loads the menu module), for the record's title and its options, and the menu's 36 more (58), counted offline with the accounting stubs.
  - **Nothing a pilot sees or does changes.** The menu and the picker build the same nodes as before -- compared node by node in every property that is not a function, at 800x480 and 480x272, with 0, 1, 4 and 8 packs, armed and disarmed, with the tuning preview on and off -- and every press, RTN on the picker included, leaves the same state on the widget and performs the same actions. `bin/accounting/measure.lua` reports every row unchanged, `--phases` included.
- **A theme that takes fullscreen can draw the quick menu's entries itself, and the widget still runs them (`widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/fullscreen_menu.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`, `docs/dashboard/quick-menu.md`)**:
  - The theme draws, the widget acts. `ctx` gains `ctx.entry(id)`, `ctx.list(name)` -- a menu is a named list of entry ids, and the widget offers one, `quick` -- `ctx.visible(entry)` and `ctx.run(entry, option, after)`, which does the entry's work, or an option's, and then what follows it, in the one place the quick menu's own buttons and the picker's now run through as well. `after` lets a theme keep its own surface up instead of the entry's follow-up. A theme adds no entry and changes none: `ctx.run` looks the entry up again by id among the menu's own records, and the option by id among that record's options (a battery profile's number, a pack's id, NO BATTERY by `none`), and runs those, never a press out of the table it was handed; an entry or option the menu does not have is refused. A list handed to `ctx.menu` likewise chooses and orders the menu's own records by id, and an id the menu does not have is not drawn.
  - What a theme can show beside an entry is read on each build: whether it is offered, which option is current, how full the blackbox is (`ctx.info`), and `ctx.status(id)` -- `busy` once the entry's messages to the flight controller are queued, `ok` once the last of them is answered, `failed` if any of them is given up. Every message of the chain gets an error handler, and an existing reply handler (the blackbox summary's) is wrapped, not replaced. A new outcome rebuilds the screen; it lapses with the stack -- `done`, `exitFullscreen`, leaving fullscreen, a reconnect -- and when another theme is selected.
  - The stack of views is now the session of one visit to fullscreen: the outcomes are kept on it, so the one assignment that drops the stack on leaving fullscreen drops them too, and closing the last view leaves an empty stack rather than none.
  - **The quick menu itself is unchanged**: it closes at once and shows no outcome, its buttons pass no reporter, and what they queue is byte for byte what they queued before -- compared message by message for every button at both layout sizes. `bin/accounting/measure.lua` reports every row unchanged, `--phases` included.
- **A free-form theme can register fullscreen views of its own, and draw the quick menu and the battery picker itself (`widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/battery_pick_menu.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`)**:
  - `views = { { id, module, openWhen, where }, ... }` in the theme's `init.lua`. An id the widget has -- `menu`, `battery_pick` -- replaces that view's look only: when it opens, what RTN does on it and what its presses are followed by stay the widget's, and the theme draws the widget's records through `ctx`. Any other id is a view of the theme's own, opened with `ctx.action("openView:<id>")`, closed by RTN unless it declares a `back(ctx)`.
  - The list is read with the `fullscreen` key, on the first fullscreen pass after the theme on screen has changed and only where the phase module on screen is free-form, so a theme load costs what it did. A view's module is loaded the first time it is built, through the theme loader -- the theme's own folder, a user theme from source -- and built `build(children, zone, state, ctx)` like the rest of the theme; one that does not load, or whose `build` raises, is not asked for again, with one log line -- a replaced look is then built from the widget's own module, and a view of the theme's own is closed. A view of the previous theme's still on the stack is taken off it, so no job is queued for a view nothing can build. The job that first loads and builds a view records its key with the module's own `renderKey` part, so a view whose module has one is built once on its first opening rather than twice.
  - The picker is the widget's for every theme that does not draw it, and draws the same record a theme draws; the sentence that a theme draws no part of it is withdrawn.
  - A theme that registers nothing is not affected: the menu and the picker build the same nodes and every press does the same as before, and `bin/accounting/measure.lua` reports every row unchanged, `--phases` included.
- **A fullscreen view opens, or comes to the top, when its condition rises (`widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`)**:
  - Every view's `openWhen` is asked on every fullscreen pass, the widget's views first and then the theme's in the order it lists them, and not only until the first one holds. A view opens when its condition RISES -- false on the last pass, true on this one; entering fullscreen with it already true counts -- and where it is already open it is brought to the top, the views above it staying open under it. A condition that merely holds forces nothing: the menu opened over a view that a switch holds open stays usable, and a view closed, or left with `done`, while its condition holds stays closed until the condition falls and rises again. A view its condition opened still closes when the condition falls.
  - Where several rise on one pass, the last in that order ends on top, so a theme's view rising together with the battery prompt lies above the picker and the picker shows again when it is closed; before, the first view whose condition held was the only one opened.
  - What each condition answered is kept on the session of the visit, so leaving fullscreen and a reconnect forget it in the assignment that already drops the stack; `done` carries what the theme's views' conditions answered into the session it starts, and not the widget's own: the battery prompt, still waiting, comes back after the menu over it is closed with its X, as it did before.
  - For the battery prompt, the only view the widget opens by itself, nothing changes: it opens on entering fullscreen while it is waiting, the menu opened over it stays above it, and every way of answering or closing it clears the condition as before. What a fullscreen pass costs, counted offline with the accounting stubs against the tree before the theme views: a legacy fullscreen pass +26 instructions with the menu up and -42 with the picker up, a theme-mode pass +22 with the theme up and +16 with the menu over it, and +94 to +129 on the first pass of a visit. The picker's pass is cheaper because the condition of a view its condition opened was asked twice per pass before and is asked once now. Zone passes and `bin/accounting/measure.lua`, `--phases` included, are unchanged.
- **A theme's view can open on a switch, on a switch the theme's settings name, or on a test of its own (`widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - `openWhen = { switch = "SA", pos = "up" | "mid" | "down" }` opens on a switch position, `{ switch = "L01" }` on a logical switch, and `{ switch = { pref = "<key>", default = "SA" }, pos = ... }` on the switch the theme's own settings name under that key, so a pilot picks the switch on the theme's settings page. A setting that holds a number is a switch position as the radio's switch picker stores it, the shape the in-flight tuning interlock already uses, and is read as it is.
  - The name is the one the radio's menus show, which is what the firmware's `getSwitchIndex` looks a position up by: the switch and an arrow for up and down or a dash for the middle, and a logical switch as `L` and two digits. It is looked up once per theme load and again when the theme's settings are saved; a pass reads one value with `getSwitchValue`. A name the radio does not know never opens the view.
  - `openWhen = function(state) ... end` is the theme's own test, called with the widget state only, on the passes where its view can be shown, under `pcall`: one that raises counts as false and is logged once per theme and view rather than on every pass.
  - What it costs: 9 instructions more per fullscreen pass for each view that has an `openWhen` -- the battery prompt's, for a theme that registers nothing -- over the entry above, counted the same way; zone passes and `bin/accounting/measure.lua`, `--phases` included, are unchanged.
- **A free-form theme can register zone views, which take the widget zone while their condition holds (`widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - A view with `where = "zone"` (or `"both"`) replaces the theme's zone picture while its `openWhen` holds -- a level, the way the in-flight tuning surface takes the zone -- armed as well as disarmed; the first of the theme's zone views whose condition holds is shown. It is display only: not on the view stack, no key, and built `build(children, zone, state)` without a `ctx`, so it binds no press. Its own `renderKey(zone, state)` is asked with the conditions.
  - The conditions are asked on the zone's 2 Hz tick -- the pass after the render-key throttle has moved -- and on no other pass, so a zone view comes and goes within about half a second. That pass marks the scene for a rebuild when the view to show changes; the scene job builds the zone view or the theme in a pass of its own, never inline. A zone view whose module does not load or whose build raises is dropped with one log line, and the theme's own zone picture is built in its place.
  - A declarative theme cannot reach any of it. A free-form theme without zone views pays only the list read: the list is read by the scene build in the branch only a free-form module takes, once per theme path, and the conditions are asked by a `refresh` wrapper that only a theme with zone views puts in place and that takes itself out once that theme is gone. `bin/accounting/measure.lua` reports `theme.urban` 11 instructions higher (worst pass 13877 to 13888, the sweep unchanged) and every other row unchanged, `--phases` included. For a theme that has them, with one zone view, the wrapper costs 18 instructions on a zone pass and 95 to 100 on the pass after each tick that asks the conditions, counted offline with the accounting stubs, armed and disarmed alike.
- **The theme check knows a theme's views (`bin/themes/validate.lua`, `bin/themes/README.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`)**:
  - The `views` of a theme's `init.lua` are checked whether or not the theme takes fullscreen. Every view's module is built -- a fullscreen view with the recording `ctx`, its presses fired and held to the same rules as the theme's own, and a zone view without a `ctx`, as the widget builds it -- and the records a view reaches through `ctx.entry` and `ctx.list` are the widget's own quick menu records; `ctx.run` looks the entry and option up again by id among them, as the widget does, and records what would follow instead of doing the work. A press that runs an entry or option the menu does not have is red.
  - A view is red where it binds a press as a zone view or has no `openWhen` there, where its `openWhen` names a condition the widget does not have, a switch none of the names a radio gives by default (`SA` to `SR`, `SW1` to `SW6`, `FL1` to `FL4`, `L01` to `L64`) or a physical switch without `pos`, or reads a setting the theme's settings page -- its `configure` module and the theme files that module loads -- does not name, as a table key, a field assignment such as `values.key =` or a quoted string, or without a `default`, and where a condition function raises or costs more than 35 instructions a call on the fixture state of any phase. The 35 is what asking the costliest of the widget's own conditions costs, counted the same way; the README gives the three figures it was taken from. A number where a switch name goes is a stored switch position, read as the widget reads it, and a setting's `default` of `0` is "no switch" and passes.
  - A press may open a view the theme registers. Every shipped theme is green, as before.
- **A theme that takes fullscreen can bind MDL, SYS and TELE (`widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `bin/themes/validate.lua`, `bin/themes/README.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`)**:
  - `ctx.keys.mdl`, `ctx.keys.sys` and `ctx.keys.tele` take an action, as `exit`, `pageDown` and `pageUp` do, and the widget performs it on the key's release edge (`EVT_MODEL_BREAK`, `EVT_SYS_BREAK`, `EVT_TELEM_BREAK`) while the theme is showing. With a view on top, and without a binding, the keys do nothing, as before: in a widget's fullscreen the radio does not open its own menus for them, because the widget gets the keys instead.
  - The keys are asked only on a pass that carries an event, after the three the widget already answers, so a pass without a key costs what it did. The theme check accepts the three names and still counts only `exit` as a way out of fullscreen, since not every radio has the three keys.
  - A key bound to `openView:<id>`, RTN aside, closes that view again when it is pressed while the view is on top, so a bound key toggles its view, where a page key would otherwise open the quick menu over it and MDL, SYS and TELE would do nothing.
- **The tool opens from the dashboard at full screen (`widgets/dashboard/tool_host.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/views.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/fullscreen_controls.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/fullscreen_menu.lua`, `widgets/dashboard/runtime.lua`, `ui/home.lua`, `bin/themes/validate.lua`, `docs/dashboard/quick-menu.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-views.md`, `docs/developer/dashboard-themes.md`)**:
  - The dashboard widget at full screen offers the suite's tool: a **MAIN MENU** entry in the quick menu, a tool control beside the menu control over a theme that takes full screen and binds no control of its own, and the same control on the connect splash, so it can be opened before the link is up. It is offered while the model is disarmed.
  - The tool runs inside the widget until it is closed, from the same `ui/home.lua` the tool script runs. It uses the widget's own MSP runtime and event runner, so the link has one client and the connect sequence is not run a second time. The back key at the top of its menu closes it; so does the model arming and leaving full screen, both through the tool's own closing sequence, so a page's queued writes still go out.
